**Emmanuel Jean-Michel Frédéric Macron**[&#8202;](#ref-back-2-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-2) (born 21 December 1977) is a French politician who has served as [President of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_France "President of France") since 2017. He has been a member of [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_(French_political_party) "Renaissance (French political party)") since founding the party in 2016. Macron previously served as [Deputy Secretary-General to the President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_President_of_France "Office of the President of France") from 2012 to 2014 and [Minister of Economics and Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Economics_and_Finance_(France) "Ministry of Economics and Finance (France)") from 2014 to 2016 under President [François Hollande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande "François Hollande").

Born in [Amiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amiens "Amiens"), Macron studied philosophy at [Paris Nanterre University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Nanterre_University "Paris Nanterre University"). He completed a [master's degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master's_degree "Master's degree") in public affairs at [Sciences Po](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sciences_Po "Sciences Po") and graduated from the [École nationale d'administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_nationale_d'administration "École nationale d'administration") in 2004. He worked as a senior civil servant at the [General Inspectorate of Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspection_g%C3%A9n%C3%A9rale_des_finances "Inspection générale des finances") and as an [investment banker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_banker "Investment banker") at [Rothschild & Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rothschild_&_Co "Rothschild & Co"). Macron was a senior adviser to President Hollande, being appointed as [Élysée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lys%C3%A9e_Palace "Élysée Palace") deputy secretary-general by Hollande after the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_French_presidential_election "2012 French presidential election"). He was later appointed as Economics Minister in 2014 in the [second Valls government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Valls_government "Second Valls government"), and led several business-friendly reforms. He resigned in 2016 to launch his 2017 presidential campaign. A member of the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_(France) "Socialist Party (France)") between 2006 and 2009, he ran in the election under the banner of [En Marche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_(French_political_party) "Renaissance (French political party)"), a [centrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centrism "Centrism") and [pro-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Europeanism "Pro-Europeanism") political movement which he founded in 2016.

Partly due to the [Fillon affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fillon_affair "Fillon affair"), Macron was [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election "2017 French presidential election") president in May 2017 with 66% of the second-round vote, defeating [Marine Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Le_Pen "Marine Le Pen") of the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally "National Rally"). Aged 39, he became the youngest president in French history. In the [2017 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_legislative_election "2017 French legislative election"), his party, renamed La République En Marche! (LREM), secured a majority in the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_(France) "National Assembly (France)"). During [his presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Emmanuel_Macron "Presidency of Emmanuel Macron"), Macron has overseen reforms to [labour laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_labour_law "French labour law"), [taxation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_France "Taxation in France"), and [pensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pensions_in_France "Pensions in France"); and pursued a [renewable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy "Renewable energy") [energy transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_transition "Energy transition"). Dubbed "president of the rich" by opponents, increasing [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_Emmanuel_Macron "Protests against Emmanuel Macron") against his reforms culminated in 2018–2020 with the [yellow vests protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_vests_protests "Yellow vests protests") and the [pension reform strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_French_pension_reform_strike "2019–2020 French pension reform strike"). From 2020, he led France's response to the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_France "COVID-19 pandemic in France") and [subsequent recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_recession "COVID-19 recession"), including by overseeing the country's [vaccination rollout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_France "COVID-19 vaccination in France"). In foreign policy, Macron called for reforms to the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union") (EU) and signed treaties [with Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aachen_Treaty "Aachen Treaty") and [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quirinal_Treaty "Quirinal Treaty"). He conducted [€](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro "Euro") 40 billion in trade and business agreements with China during the [China–United States trade war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93United_States_trade_war "China–United States trade war") and oversaw a dispute with Australia and the US over the [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS "AUKUS") security pact. Macron has also continued [Opération _Chammal_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ration_Chammal "Opération Chammal") in the [war against the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_against_the_Islamic_State "War against the Islamic State") and joined in the [international condemnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_and_intergovernmental_reactions_to_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "Government and intergovernmental reactions to the Russian invasion of Ukraine") of the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine").

Macron was elected to a second term in the [2022 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_presidential_election "2022 French presidential election"), again defeating Le Pen and becoming the first French presidential candidate to win reelection since [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac "Jacques Chirac") in [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_French_presidential_election "2002 French presidential election"). In the [2022 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_legislative_election "2022 French legislative election"), Macron's [centrist coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ensemble_(coalition) "Ensemble (coalition)") lost its majority, resulting in a [hung parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hung_parliament "Hung parliament") and formation of France's first [minority government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_government "Minority government") since [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_French_legislative_elections "1993 French legislative elections"). In 2023, the [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borne_government "Borne government") of his prime minister, [Élisabeth Borne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lisabeth_Borne "Élisabeth Borne"), passed [legislation raising the retirement age from 62 to 64](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_French_pension_reform_bill "2023 French pension reform bill"); this led to [public sector strikes and violent protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_French_pension_reform_strikes "2023 French pension reform strikes"). In 2024, Macron appointed [Gabriel Attal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Attal "Gabriel Attal") as prime minister after [a government crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023%E2%80%932024_French_government_crisis "2023–2024 French government crisis"). He then dissolved the National Assembly and called a [snap legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_French_legislative_election "2024 French legislative election") following overwhelming defeat at the [2024 European Parliament elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_European_Parliament_election_in_France "2024 European Parliament election in France"), which resulted in another hung parliament and an electoral defeat for his coalition. Afterwards, Macron appointed [Michel Barnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Barnier "Michel Barnier"), a [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism "Gaullism") and former chief [Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit "Brexit") negotiator, as prime minister. Three months in, [Barnier was toppled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Barnier_government "Collapse of the Barnier government") by a historic [vote of no confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence "Motion of no confidence"), prompting Macron to replace him with centrist veteran [François Bayrou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Bayrou "François Bayrou"). After Bayrou was [himself brought down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayrou_government#Collapse "Bayrou government") by a confidence vote in September 2025, Macron appointed [Sébastien Lecornu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9bastien_Lecornu "Sébastien Lecornu"), [Minister of the Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Armed_Forces_(France) "Minister of the Armed Forces (France)"), as prime minister. Lecornu [resigned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Lecornu_government#Resignation "First Lecornu government") less than a month later following political backlash over the composition of his government, but was reappointed by Macron shortly afterwards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-3-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-3) The formations of these short-lived governments marked [a political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_French_political_crisis "2024–2025 French political crisis").

## Early life

Emmanuel Jean-Michel Frédéric Macron was born on 21 December 1977 in [Amiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amiens "Amiens"). He is the son of Françoise Macron (née Noguès), a physician, and [Jean-Michel Macron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Michel_Macron "Jean-Michel Macron"), professor of [neurology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurology "Neurology") at the [University of Picardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Picardy "University of Picardy").[&#8202;](#ref-back-4-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-4)[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-5) The couple divorced in 2010. He has two siblings, Laurent, born in 1979, and Estelle, born in 1982. Françoise and Jean-Michel's first child was stillborn.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-6)

The Macron family legacy is traced back to the village of [Authie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authie,_Somme "Authie, Somme"), [Picardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picardy "Picardy").[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-7) One of his paternal great-grandfathers, George William Robertson, was English, and was born in [Bristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol "Bristol"), United Kingdom.[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-9) His maternal grandparents, Jean and Germaine Noguès (née Arribet), are from the [Pyrenean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrenees "Pyrenees") town of [Bagnères-de-Bigorre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagn%C3%A8res-de-Bigorre "Bagnères-de-Bigorre"), [Gascony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gascony "Gascony").[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-10) He commonly visited Bagnères-de-Bigorre to visit his grandmother Germaine, whom he called "Manette".[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-11) Macron associates his enjoyment of reading[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-12) and his leftward political leanings to Germaine, who, after coming from a modest upbringing of a stationmaster father and a housekeeping mother, became a teacher and then a principal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-13)

Although raised in a non-religious family, Macron was baptised a [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") at his own request at age 12; he is [agnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnostic_atheism "Agnostic atheism") today.[&#8202;](#ref-back-lacroixgorcelajeunessetres-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-lacroixgorcelajeunessetres) His parents chose his name in reference to the biblical prophecy of Jesus Christ, [Emmanuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel "Immanuel").[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-15)

Macron was educated mainly at the Jesuit institute [Lycée la Providence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyc%C3%A9e_la_Providence "Lycée la Providence")[&#8202;](#ref-back-galabio-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-galabio) in Amiens[&#8202;](#ref-back-france24-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-france24) before his parents sent him to finish his last year of school[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chrisafis-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-Chrisafis) at the elite [Lycée Henri-IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyc%C3%A9e_Henri-IV "Lycée Henri-IV") in Paris, where he completed the [high school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyc%C3%A9e "Lycée") curriculum and the [undergraduate program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kh%C3%A2gne "Khâgne") with a "Bac S, Mention Très bien". At the same time, he was nominated for the " [Concours général](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concours_g%C3%A9n%C3%A9ral "Concours général")" (most selective national level high school competition) in French literature and received his diploma for his piano studies at Amiens Conservatory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Neva_Editions_2015,_p.193-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-Neva_Editions_2015,_p.193) His parents sent him to Paris due to their alarm at the bond he had formed with [Brigitte Auzière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigitte_Macron "Brigitte Macron"), a married teacher with three children at Jésuites de la Providence, who later became his wife.[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-20)

In Paris, Macron twice failed to gain entry to the [École normale supérieure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_normale_sup%C3%A9rieure_(Paris) "École normale supérieure (Paris)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Vincent_de_Féligonde-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-Vincent_de_Féligonde)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-22)[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-23) He instead studied philosophy at the [University of Paris-Ouest Nanterre La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris_X "University of Paris X"), obtaining a [DEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dipl%C3%B4me_d'%C3%89tudes_Approfondies "Diplôme d'Études Approfondies") degree (a [master level degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master's_degree_in_Europe "Master's degree in Europe")), with a thesis on [Machiavelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niccol%C3%B2_Machiavelli "Niccolò Machiavelli") and [Hegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Friedrich_Hegel "Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel").[&#8202;](#ref-back-galabio-1)[\[14\]](#refnote-galabio)[&#8202;](#ref-back-lefigdejaeger-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-lefigdejaeger) Around 1999 Macron worked as an editorial assistant to [Paul Ricoeur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Ricoeur "Paul Ricoeur"), the [French Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huguenots "Huguenots") philosopher who was then writing his last major work, _La Mémoire, l'Histoire, l'Oubli_. Macron worked mainly on the notes and bibliography.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Guélaud-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-Guélaud)[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-26) Macron became a member of the editorial board of the literary magazine _[Esprit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esprit_(magazine) "Esprit (magazine)")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-27)

Macron did not perform [national service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_France "Conscription in France") because he was pursuing his graduate studies. Born in December 1977, he belonged to the last cohort for whom military service was mandatory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-28)

Macron obtained a [master's degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master's_degree_in_Europe "Master's degree in Europe") in [public affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_administration "Public administration") at [Sciences Po](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_d'%C3%A9tudes_politiques_de_Paris "Institut d'études politiques de Paris"), majoring in "Public Guidance and Economy" before training for a senior civil service career at the selective [École nationale d'administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_nationale_d'administration "École nationale d'administration") (ENA), training at the French Embassy in [Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria "Nigeria")[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-29) and at the [prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefectures_in_France "Prefectures in France") of [Oise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oise "Oise") before graduating in 2004.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kaplan-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-kaplan)

## Professional career

### Inspector of Finances

After graduating from ENA in 2004, Macron became an Inspector in the [_Inspection générale des finances_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspection_g%C3%A9n%C3%A9rale_des_finances_(France) "Inspection générale des finances (France)") (IGF), a branch of the [Finance Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Ministry_for_the_Economy_and_Finance "French Ministry for the Economy and Finance").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Guélaud-1)[\[23\]](#refnote-Guélaud) Macron was mentored by [Jean-Pierre Jouyet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Jouyet "Jean-Pierre Jouyet"), the then-head of the IGF.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-31) During his time as an Inspector of Finances, Macron gave lectures during the summer at the "prep'ENA" (a special cram school for the ENA entrance examination) at [IPESUP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_priv%C3%A9_de_pr%C3%A9paration_aux_%C3%A9tudes_sup%C3%A9rieures?action=edit&redlink=1 "Institut privé de préparation aux études supérieures (page does not exist)"), an elite private school specializing in preparation for the entrance examinations of the _[Grandes écoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandes_%C3%A9coles "Grandes écoles")_, such as [HEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HEC_Paris "HEC Paris") or Sciences Po.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-32)[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-33)[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-34)

In 2006, [Laurence Parisot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurence_Parisot "Laurence Parisot") offered him the job of managing director for _[Mouvement des Entreprises de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mouvement_des_Entreprises_de_France "Mouvement des Entreprises de France")_, the largest [employer federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employer_federation "Employer federation") in France, but he declined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-35)

In August 2007, Macron was appointed deputy rapporteur for [Jacques Attali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Attali "Jacques Attali")'s "Commission to Unleash French Growth".[&#8202;](#ref-back-france24-1)[\[15\]](#refnote-france24) In 2008, Macron paid €50,000 to buy himself out of his government contract.[&#8202;](#ref-back-marnham-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-marnham) He then became an investment banker in a highly-paid position at [Rothschild & Cie Banque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rothschild_&_Cie_Banque "Rothschild & Cie Banque").[&#8202;](#ref-back-macronbio-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-macronbio)[&#8202;](#ref-back-lepoint-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-lepoint) In March 2010, he was appointed to the Attali Commission as a member.[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-39)

### Investment banker

In September 2008, Macron left his job as an Inspector of Finances and took a position at [Rothschild & Cie Banque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rothschild_&_Co "Rothschild & Co").[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-40) Macron left the government when [Nicolas Sarkozy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Sarkozy "Nicolas Sarkozy") became president. He was originally offered the job by François Henrot. His first responsibility at the bank was assisting with the acquisition of [Cofidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cofidis "Cofidis") by [Crédit Mutuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cr%C3%A9dit_Mutuel "Crédit Mutuel") Nord Europe.[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-41)

Macron formed a relationship with [Alain Minc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Minc "Alain Minc"), a businessman on the supervisory board of _[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde "Le Monde")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-42) In 2010, Macron was promoted to partner with the bank after working on the recapitalization of _Le Monde_ and the acquisition by [Atos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atos "Atos") of [Siemens IT Solutions and Services.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atos_Information_Technology_Incorporated "Atos Information Technology Incorporated")[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-43) In the same year, Macron was put in charge of [Nestlé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestl%C3%A9 "Nestlé")'s acquisition of Pfizer's infant nutrition division for €9 billion, which made him a millionaire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-44)[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-45)

In February 2012, Macron advised businessman [Philippe Tillous-Borde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Tillous-Borde "Philippe Tillous-Borde"), the CEO of the [Avril Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avril_Group "Avril Group").[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-46)

Macron reported that he had earned €2 million between December 2010 and May 2012.[&#8202;](#ref-back-L'Express-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-L'Express) Official documents show that between 2009 and 2013, Macron had earned almost €3 million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-48) He left Rothschild & Cie in 2012.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-49)[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-50)

## Early political career

In his youth beginning in 1998, Macron worked for the [Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizen_and_Republican_Movement "Citizen and Republican Movement") for two years, but he never applied to be a member.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marianne-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-Marianne)[&#8202;](#ref-back-L'Express-1)[\[45\]](#refnote-L'Express) Macron was an assistant for Mayor [Georges Sarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Sarre "Georges Sarre") of the [11th arrondissement of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11th_arrondissement_of_Paris "11th arrondissement of Paris") during his time at Sciences Po.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-52) Macron joined the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_(France) "Socialist Party (France)") at the age of 24,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Libération-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-Libération) but last renewed his membership for the period 2006–2009.[&#8202;](#ref-back-scan-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-scan)

Macron met [François Hollande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande "François Hollande") through Jean-Pierre Jouyet in 2006 and joined his staff in 2010.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Libération-1)[\[51\]](#refnote-Libération) In 2007, Macron attempted to run for a seat in the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_(France) "National Assembly (France)") in Picardy under the Socialist Party label in the [2007 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_French_legislative_election "2007 French legislative election"); however, his application was declined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-55) Macron was offered the chance to be the deputy chief of staff to Prime Minister [François Fillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Fillon "François Fillon") in 2010, though he declined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-56)

### Deputy Secretary-General of the Élysée (2012–2014)

On 15 May 2012, Macron became the deputy secretary-general of the [Élysée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lys%C3%A9e_Palace "Élysée Palace"), a senior role in President François Hollande's staff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-57)[&#8202;](#ref-back-kaplan-1)[\[28\]](#refnote-kaplan) Macron served with [Nicolas Revel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Revel "Nicolas Revel"). He served under the secretary-general, [Pierre-René Lemas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre-Ren%C3%A9_Lemas "Pierre-René Lemas").

During the summer of 2012, Macron put forward a proposal that would increase the 35-hour work week to 37 hours until 2014. He also tried to hold back the large tax increases on the highest earners that were planned by the government. Hollande refused Macron's proposals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-58) In 2013, his was one of the deciding votes against regulating the salaries of [CEOs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_executive_officer "Chief executive officer").[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-59) Nicolas Revel, the other deputy secretary-general of the Élysée opposed Macron on a proposed budget responsibility pact favoured by the [Medef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mouvement_des_Entreprises_de_France "Mouvement des Entreprises de France").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berretta-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-Berretta)

On 10 June 2014, it was announced that Macron had resigned from his role and was replaced by [Laurence Boone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurence_Boone "Laurence Boone").[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-61) Reasons given for his departure included his disappointment at not being included in the [first Government of Manuel Valls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Valls_government "First Valls government") and his frustration with his lack of influence on the reforms proposed by the government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Berretta-1)[\[58\]](#refnote-Berretta) This was following the appointment of Jean-Pierre Jouyet as chief of staff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-L'Obs-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-L'Obs)

Jouyet said that Macron left to "continue personal aspirations"[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-63) and create his own financial consultancy firm.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-64) It was later reported that he was planning to create an investment firm that would attempt to fund educational projects.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Marianne-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-Marianne) Shortly afterwards he was hired as a [research fellow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_fellow "Research fellow") at the [University of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humboldt_University_of_Berlin "Humboldt University of Berlin") with the help of businessman Alain Minc. He had also sought a position at [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University "Harvard University").[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-65)

Offered a chance to be a candidate in the municipal elections in 2014 in his hometown of Amiens, Macron declined,[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-66) leading François Hollande to reject Manuel Valls's idea of appointing him Budget Minister, as he had never been elected to public office.[&#8202;](#ref-back-L'Obs-1)[\[60\]](#refnote-L'Obs)

### Minister of Economics and Finance (2014–2017)

See also: [Uber Files](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uber_Files "Uber Files")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/Emmanuel_Macron_%2827_ao%C3%BBt_2014%29.jpg/330px-Emmanuel_Macron_%2827_ao%C3%BBt_2014%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Emmanuel_Macron_(27_ao%C3%BBt_2014).jpg) Macron as the [Minister of Economics and Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Economy_and_Finance_(France) "Ministry of Economy and Finance (France)")

He was appointed as the Minister of Economics and Finance in the [Second Valls government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Valls_government "Second Valls government") on 26 August 2014, replacing [Arnaud Montebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnaud_Montebourg "Arnaud Montebourg").[&#8202;](#ref-back-corbet-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-corbet) He was the youngest Minister of Economics since [Valéry Giscard d'Estaing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val%C3%A9ry_Giscard_d'Estaing "Valéry Giscard d'Estaing") in 1962.[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-68) Macron was branded by the media as the "Anti-Montebourg" due to being pro-EU and much more liberal, while Montebourg was [eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroscepticism "Euroscepticism") and left-wing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-lejdd-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-lejdd) As Minister of Economics, Macron was at the forefront of pushing through business-friendly reforms. On 17 February 2015, prime minister [Manuel Valls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Valls "Manuel Valls") pushed Macron's signature law package through a reluctant parliament using the special [49.3 procedure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49.3_(France) "49.3 (France)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Revault_d'Allonnes-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-Revault_d'Allonnes)

Macron increased the French share in the company [Renault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renault "Renault") from 15% to 20% and then enforced the _Florange law_ which grants double voting rights on shares registered for more than two years unless two-thirds of shareholders vote to overturn it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-71) This gave the French state a minority share in the company though Macron later stated that the government would limit its powers within Renault.[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-72)

Macron was widely criticized for being unable to prevent the closing down of an Ecopla factory in [Isère](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Is%C3%A8re "Isère").[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-73)

In August 2015, Macron said that he was no longer a member of the Socialist Party and was an independent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-scan-1)[\[52\]](#refnote-scan)

#### Macron Law

A law which had originally been sponsored by [Arnaud Montebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnaud_Montebourg "Arnaud Montebourg") before he left the government, and which had focused on "purchasing power", grew into the [Macron law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macron_law?action=edit&redlink=1 "Macron law (page does not exist)")\[ [fr](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loi%20pour%20la%20croissance,%20l'activit%C3%A9%20et%20l'%C3%A9galit%C3%A9%20des%20chances%20%C3%A9conomiques "fr:Loi pour la croissance, l'activité et l'égalité des chances économiques")\], a grab bag of measures liberalizing laws prohibiting work on Sunday and at night; restrictions on coaches for public transportation; regulations for debt collectors, barristers and auctioneers; and rules governing the rental of equipment by the military from private companies. The law also sought to simplify many government procedures, such as that for obtaining a driving licence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-74)[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-75) Manuel Valls, fearing that it would not pass in the National Assembly, decided to push the law through with the 49.3 procedure[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-76)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Revault_d'Allonnes-1)[\[68\]](#refnote-Revault_d'Allonnes) and so it was adopted on 10 April 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-77)

Estimates of the increase in GDP the law might generate ranged from 0.3% to 0.5%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-78)[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-79)

### 2017 presidential campaign

Main article: [2017 French presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election "2017 French presidential election")

#### Formation of En Marche and resignation from government

Macron first became known to the French public after his appearance on the French TV programme _Des Paroles Et Des Actes_ in March 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-80) Before forming his political party [En Marche!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_(French_political_party) "Renaissance (French political party)"), he gave a number of speeches, his first one in March 2015 in [Val-de-Marne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val-de-Marne "Val-de-Marne").[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-81) He threatened to leave Manuel Valls' [second government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Valls_government "Second Valls government") over the proposed removal of dual-nationality from terrorists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-82)[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-83) He also took various foreign trips, including one to Israel where he spoke on the advancement of digital technology.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-84)

Tensions around the question of Macron's loyalty to the Valls government and Hollande increased when they turned down a bill he put forward dubbed "Macron 2", which had a larger scope than his original law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-orange-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-orange)[&#8202;](#ref-back-L'Opinion-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-L'Opinion) Macron was given the chance to help draft into the [El Khomri law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Khomri_law "El Khomri law") and put specific parts of "Macron 2" into the law though El Khomri was able to overturn these with the help of other ministers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-87)

Amid tensions and deterioration of relations with the current government, Macron founded an independent political party, En Marche, in Amiens on 6 April 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-88) A [social-liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social-liberal "Social-liberal"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-unBanquier-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-unBanquier) [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism "Progressivism")[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-90)[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-91) political movement that gathered huge media coverage when it was first established,[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-92) the party and Macron were both reprimanded by President Hollande and the question of Macron's loyalty to the government was raised.[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-93)[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-94) Several MEPs spoke out in support for the movement[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-95) though the majority of the Socialist Party spoke against En Marche including [Manuel Valls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Valls "Manuel Valls"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-brustier-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-brustier) [Michel Sapin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Sapin "Michel Sapin"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-97) [Axelle Lemaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axelle_Lemaire "Axelle Lemaire") and [Christian Eckert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Eckert "Christian Eckert").[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-98)

In June 2016, support for Macron and his movement, En Marche, began to grow in the media with _[Libération](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lib%C3%A9ration "Libération")_ reporting that _[L'Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Express "L'Express")_, _[Les Échos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_%C3%89chos_(France) "Les Échos (France)")_, [Le 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_1?action=edit&redlink=1 "Le 1 (page does not exist)")\[ [fr](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le%201 "fr:Le 1")\], and _[L'Opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Opinion_(newspaper) "L'Opinion (newspaper)")_ had begun to support him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-99) Following several controversies surrounding trade unionists and their protests, Acrimed reported that major newspapers began to run front-page stories about Macron and En Marche.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-100) Criticized by both the far-left and the far-right, these pro-Macron influencers in the press were dubbed "Macronites".[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-101)[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-102)

In May 2016, [Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orleans "Orleans") mayor [Olivier Carré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Carr%C3%A9 "Olivier Carré") invited Macron to the festival commemorating the 587th anniversary of [Joan of Arc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_of_Arc "Joan of Arc")'s efforts during the [Siege of Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Orl%C3%A9ans_(1428%E2%80%931429) "Siege of Orléans (1428–1429)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-103)[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-104) [LCI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Cha%C3%AEne_Info "La Chaîne Info") reported that Macron was trying to take back the symbol of Joan of Arc from the far-right.[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-105) Macron later went to [Puy du Fou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puy_du_Fou "Puy du Fou") and declared he was "not a socialist" in a speech amid rumours he was going to leave the current government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-106)
On 30 August 2016, Macron resigned from the government ahead of the [2017 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election "2017 French presidential election"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc-macron-quits-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-bbc-macron-quits)[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-108) to devote himself to his En Marche movement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-109)[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-110) There had been rising tensions and several reports that he had wanted to leave the Valls government since early 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-111) He initially planned to leave after the cancellation\[ _[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] of his "Macron 2" law[&#8202;](#ref-back-L'Opinion-1)[\[84\]](#refnote-L'Opinion) but decided to stay on temporarily after a meeting with President François Hollande.[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-112) Michel Sapin was announced as Macron's replacement,[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-113) while Hollande said he felt Macron had "methodically betrayed" him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-114) An [IFOP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_fran%C3%A7ais_d'opinion_publique "Institut français d'opinion publique") poll showed that 84% of those surveyed agreed with his decision to resign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-115)

#### First round of the presidential election

Macron first showed his intention to run by forming En Marche, but following his resignation from the government, he was able to dedicate more time to his movement. He first announced that he was considering running for president in April 2016,[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-116) and after his resignation from the position of economy minister, media sources began to identify patterns in his fundraising indicating he would run.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-117) In October 2016, Macron criticized Hollande's goal of being a "normal" president, saying that France needed a more " [Jupiterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter_(mythology) "Jupiter (mythology)") presidency".[&#8202;](#ref-back-jupiter-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-jupiter)

On 16 November 2016, Macron formally declared his candidacy for the French presidency after months of speculation. In his announcement speech, he called for a "democratic revolution" and promised to "unblock France".[&#8202;](#ref-back-centrist1-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-centrist1) He had expressed hope that Hollande would run several months earlier, saying that—as the sitting president—he was the legitimate Socialist party candidate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-120)[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-121) Macron's book _Révolution_ was published on 24 November 2016 and reached fifth position on the French best-seller list in December 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-122)

Shortly after announcing his run, [Jean-Christophe Cambadélis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Christophe_Cambad%C3%A9lis "Jean-Christophe Cambadélis") and [Manuel Valls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Valls "Manuel Valls") both asked Macron to run in the [Socialist Party presidential primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Socialist_Party_presidential_primary,_2017 "French Socialist Party presidential primary, 2017"), which he ultimately refused to do.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-123)[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-124) Jean-Christophe Cambadélis began to threaten to exclude members who associated or supported Macron following [Lyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyon "Lyon") mayor [Gérard Collomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9rard_Collomb "Gérard Collomb") throwing his support behind him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-125)

Macron's campaign, headed by French economist [Sophie Ferracci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophie_Ferracci "Sophie Ferracci"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-decodeurs-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-decodeurs) announced in December 2016 that it had raised 3.7 million euros in donations,[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-127) three times the budget of then-front runner [Alain Juppé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Jupp%C3%A9 "Alain Juppé").[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-128) Macron came under criticism from several individuals, including [Benoît Hamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beno%C3%AEt_Hamon "Benoît Hamon") −who requested he reveal a list of his donors and accused him of conflicts of interest due to the time he spent at Rothschilds,[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-129) which Macron dismissed as "demagogy".[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-130) Journalists Marion L'Hour and Frédéric Says later reported that he had spent €120,000 on setting up dinners and meetings with various personalities within the media and in French popular culture while at Bercy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roger-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-Roger) [Christian Jacob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Jacob_(politician) "Christian Jacob (politician)") and [Philippe Vigier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Vigier "Philippe Vigier") accused him of using this money to campaign without campaigning.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-132) His successor, Michel Sapin, saw nothing illegal about his actions, saying that he had the right to spend the funds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-133) Macron called the allegations "defamatory" and said that none of the ministerial budget had been spent on his party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Roger-1)[\[129\]](#refnote-Roger)

Macron's campaign enjoyed considerable coverage from the media.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-134) Mediapart reported that over fifty magazine covers were dedicated purely to him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-135) Friends with the owners of Le Monde[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-136) and Claude Perdiel, the former owner of [Nouvel Observateur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Obs "L'Obs"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-137) he was labelled the "media candidate" by the far-left and far-right and was viewed as such in opinion polls.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Libération.fr-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-Libération.fr)[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-139)[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-140) Many observers compared his campaign to a product being sold[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-141) due to [Maurice Lévy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_L%C3%A9vy_(Publicis) "Maurice Lévy (Publicis)"), a former [Publicis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicis "Publicis") CEO, using marketing tactics to try to advance his presidential ambitions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-142)[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-143) The magazine _Marianne_ reported that [BFM TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BFM_TV "BFM TV"), owned by [Patrick Drahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Drahi "Patrick Drahi"), broadcast more coverage of Macron than of all the other main candidates combined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-144) _Marianne_ speculated that this might be due to the campaigns links with Drahi through [Bernard Mourad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Mourad?action=edit&redlink=1 "Bernard Mourad (page does not exist)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-145)[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-146)

[François Bayrou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Bayrou "François Bayrou"), with whom Macron had been compared, announced he was not going to stand in the presidential election and instead formed an electoral alliance with Macron,[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-147)[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-148) whose poll ratings began to rise. After several legal issues surrounding François Fillon were publicized, Macron overtook him in the polls to become the front-runner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-149)[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-150)

Macron attracted criticism for the time taken to spell out a formal program during his campaign; despite declaring in November that he had still not released a complete set of proposals by February, attracting both attacks from critics and concern among allies and supporters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-151) He eventually laid out his 150-page formal program on 2 March, publishing it online and discussing it at a marathon press conference that day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-152)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/French_Election-_Celebrations_at_The_Louvre%2C_Paris_%2833707026433%29.jpg/250px-French_Election-_Celebrations_at_The_Louvre%2C_Paris_%2833707026433%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:French_Election-_Celebrations_at_The_Louvre,_Paris_(33707026433).jpg) Macron's supporters celebrating his victory at the [Louvre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louvre "Louvre") on 7 May 2017

Macron accumulated a wide array of supporters, securing endorsements from François Bayrou of the [Democratic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Movement_(France) "Democratic Movement (France)") (MoDem), MEP [Daniel Cohn-Bendit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Cohn-Bendit "Daniel Cohn-Bendit"), the ecologist candidate [François de Rugy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_de_Rugy "François de Rugy") of the [primary of the left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Socialist_Party_presidential_primary,_2017 "French Socialist Party presidential primary, 2017"), and Socialist MP [Richard Ferrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Ferrand "Richard Ferrand"), secretary-general of En Marche, as well as numerous others – many of them from the Socialist Party, but also a significant number of centrist and centre-right politicians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-153) The [Grand Mosque of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mosque_of_Paris "Grand Mosque of Paris") urged [French Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_France "Islam in France") to vote en masse for Macron.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-154)

On 23 April 2017, Macron received the most votes in the first round of the presidential election, with more than 8 million votes (24%) and faced [Marine Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Le_Pen "Marine Le Pen") in the second round, with the support of former candidates François Fillon and Benoît Hamon[&#8202;](#ref-back-Le_Parisien-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-Le_Parisien) and the sitting president François Hollande.[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-156)

#### Second round of the presidential election

Many foreign politicians supported Macron in his bid against right-wing populist candidate [Marine Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Le_Pen "Marine Le Pen"), including [European Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Commission "European Commission") President [Jean-Claude Juncker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Claude_Juncker "Jean-Claude Juncker"), German Chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel "Angela Merkel"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-157) and former US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama").[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-158)

A debate was arranged between Macron and Le Pen on 3 May 2017. The debate lasted for two hours and opinion polls showed that he was perceived to have won.[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-159)

In March 2017, Macron's digital campaign manager, [Mounir Mahjoubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mounir_Mahjoubi "Mounir Mahjoubi"), told Britain's [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News "Sky News") that Russia is behind "high level attacks" on Macron, and said that its state media are "the first source of false information". He said: "We are accusing [RT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RT_(TV_network) "RT (TV network)") (formerly known as Russia Today) and [Sputnik News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_(news_agency) "Sputnik (news agency)") (of being) the first source of false information shared about our candidate...".[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-160)

Two days before the French presidential election on 7 May, it was reported that nine gigabytes of [Macron's campaign emails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Macron_e-mail_leaks "2017 Macron e-mail leaks") had been anonymously posted to [Pastebin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastebin.com "Pastebin.com"), a document-sharing site. These documents were then spread onto the imageboard [4chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4chan "4chan"), which led to the hashtag "#macronleaks" trending on Twitter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-161)[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-162) In a statement the same evening, Macron's political movement, En Marche, said: "The En Marche movement has been the victim of a massive and coordinated hack this evening which has given rise to the diffusion on social media of various internal information".[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-163) Macron's campaign had previously been presented a report in March 2017 by the Japanese cyber security firm [Trend Micro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trend_Micro "Trend Micro") detailing how [En Marche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/En_Marche! "En Marche!") had been the target of phishing attacks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-magazine-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-magazine) Trend Micro said that the group conducting these attacks was the Russian hacking group [Fancy Bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fancy_Bear "Fancy Bear"), also accused of hacking the [Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee "Democratic National Committee") on [22 July 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Democratic_National_Committee_email_leak "2016 Democratic National Committee email leak").[&#8202;](#ref-back-magazine-1)[\[162\]](#refnote-magazine) 21,075 verified emails and another 50,773 emails it could not verify were [released in July 2017 by WikiLeaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_material_published_by_WikiLeaks#2017_Macron_email_leak "List of material published by WikiLeaks").[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-165) This followed Le Pen accusing Macron of tax avoidance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-166)

On 7 May 2017, Macron was elected President of France with 66.1% of the vote to Marine Le Pen's 33.9%. The election had record abstention at 25.4%, and 8% of ballots were blank or spoiled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-167) Macron resigned from his role as president of En Marche[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-168) and Catherine Barbaroux became interim leader.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-169)

## Presidency (2017–present)

Main article: [Presidency of Emmanuel Macron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Emmanuel_Macron "Presidency of Emmanuel Macron")

### First term (2017–2022)

Macron formally became president on 14 May.[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-170) At 39, he became the youngest president in French history and the youngest French head of state since [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon "Napoleon").[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-171)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Napoleon-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-Napoleon) He is also the first president of France born after the establishment of the [Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fifth_Republic "French Fifth Republic") in 1958.

He appointed [Patrick Strzoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Strzoda "Patrick Strzoda") as his chief of staff[&#8202;](#ref-back-france3grammontstrzoda-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-france3grammontstrzoda) and [Ismaël Emelien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma%C3%ABl_Emelien "Ismaël Emelien") as his special advisor for strategy, communication and speeches.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bfmtvleparcours-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-bfmtvleparcours) On 15 May, he appointed [Édouard Philippe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89douard_Philippe "Édouard Philippe") of [the Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_(France) "The Republicans (France)") as [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_France "Prime Minister of France").[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-175)[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-176) On the same day, he made his first official foreign visit, meeting in Berlin with Angela Merkel, the [Chancellor of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_Germany "Chancellor of Germany"). The two leaders emphasised the importance of [France–Germany relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France%E2%80%93Germany_relations "France–Germany relations") to the European Union.[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-177) They agreed to draw up a "common road map" for Europe, insisting that neither was against changes to the [Treaties of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaties_of_the_European_Union "Treaties of the European Union").[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-178)

In the [2017 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_legislative_election "2017 French legislative election"), Macron's party La République En Marche and its Democratic Movement allies secured a comfortable majority, winning 350 seats out of 577.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-179) After the Republicans emerged as the winners of the [Senate elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_Senate_election "2017 French Senate election"), government spokesman Christophe Castaner stated the elections were a "failure" for his party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-180)

On 3 July 2020, Macron appointed the [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_politics "Centre-right politics") [Jean Castex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Castex "Jean Castex") as the Prime Minister of France. Castex, described as a [social conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism "Social conservatism"), was a member of [the Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_R%C3%A9publicains "Les Républicains").[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-181) The appointment was described as "doubling down on a course that is widely seen as centre-right in economic terms".[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-182)

#### Domestic policy

In his first few months as president, Macron pressed for the enactment of a package of reforms on public ethics, labour laws, taxes, and law enforcement agency powers.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

#### Anti-corruption

In response to [Penelopegate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fillon_affair "Fillon affair"), the National Assembly passed a part of Macron's proposed law to stop mass corruption in French politics by July 2017, banning elected representatives from hiring family members.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-183) Meanwhile, the second part of the law scrapping a constituency fund was scheduled for voting after Senate objections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-184)

Macron's plan to give his wife an official role within government came under fire with criticisms ranging from its being undemocratic to what critics perceive as a contradiction to his fight against [nepotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepotism "Nepotism").[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-185) Following an online petition of nearly 290,000 signatures on [change.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Change.org "Change.org") Macron abandoned the plan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-186) On 9 August, the National Assembly adopted the bill on public ethics, a key theme of Macron's campaign, after debates on the scrapping the constituency funds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-187)

#### Labour policy and unions

Macron aims to shift union-management relations away from the adversarial lines of the current French system and toward a more flexible, consensus-driven system modelled after [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_Germany "Trade unions in Germany") and Scandinavia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Briacon-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-Briacon)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Alderman-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-Alderman) He has also pledged to act against companies employing cheaper labour from eastern Europe and in return affecting jobs of French workers, what he has termed as " [social dumping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_dumping "Social dumping")". Under the [Posted Workers Directive 1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posted_Workers_Directive_1996 "Posted Workers Directive 1996"), eastern European workers can be employed for a limited time at the salary level in eastern European countries, which has led to dispute between the EU states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-190)

The French government announced the proposed changes to France's labour rules ("Code du Travail"), being among the first steps taken by Macron and his government to galvanize the French economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYtimetest-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-NYtimetest) Macron's reform efforts have encountered resistance from some French trade unions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-192) The largest trade union, the [CFDT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFDT "CFDT"), has taken a conciliatory approach to Macron's push and has engaged in negotiations with the president, while the more militant [CGT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Confederation_of_Labour_(France) "General Confederation of Labour (France)") is more hostile to reforms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Briacon-1)[\[186\]](#refnote-Briacon)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Alderman-1)[\[187\]](#refnote-Alderman) Macron's labour minister, [Muriel Pénicaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muriel_P%C3%A9nicaud "Muriel Pénicaud"), is overseeing the effort.[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-193)

The National Assembly, including the Senate approved the proposal, allowing the government to loosen the labour laws after negotiations with unions and employers' groups.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-194) The reforms, which were discussed with unions, limit payouts for dismissals deemed unfair and give companies greater freedom to hire and fire employees as well as to define acceptable working conditions. The president signed five [decrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9cret "Décret") reforming the labour rules on 22 September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-195) Government figures released in October 2017 revealed that during the legislative push to reform the labour code, the unemployment rate had dropped 1.8%, the biggest since 2001.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-196)

##### Migrant crisis

Speaking on refugees and, specifically, the [Calais Jungle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calais_Jungle "Calais Jungle"), Macron said on 16 January 2018 that he would not allow another refugee camp to form in Paris before outlining the government policy towards immigration and asylum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-197) He has also announced plans to speed up asylum applications and deportations but give refugees better housing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-198)

On 23 June 2018, President Macron said: "The reality is that Europe is not experiencing a migration crisis of the same magnitude as the one it experienced in 2015", "a country like Italy has not at all the same migratory pressure as last year. The crisis we are experiencing today in Europe is a political crisis".[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-199) In November 2019, Macron introduced new immigration rules to restrict the number of refugees reaching France, while stating to " [take back control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Take_back_control "Take back control")" of the immigration policy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-200)[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-201)

In 2022, the total number of new foreigners coming to France rose above 320,000 for the first time, with nearly a majority coming from [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa "Africa").[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-202)

##### Economic policy

[Pierre de Villiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_de_Villiers "Pierre de Villiers"), then-Chief of the General Staff of the Armies, stepped down on 19 July 2017 following a confrontation with Macron.[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-203) De Villiers cited the military budget cut of €850 million as the main reason he was stepping down. _Le Monde_ later reported that De Villiers told a parliamentary group, "I will not let myself be fucked like this."[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-204) Macron named [François Lecointre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Lecointre "François Lecointre") as De Villiers' replacement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-205)

Macron's government presented its first budget on 27 September, the terms of which reduced taxes as well as spending to bring the public deficit in line with the EU's fiscal rules.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Budget-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-Budget) The budget replaced the [wealth tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_tax "Wealth tax") with one targeting real estate, fulfilling Macron's campaign pledge to scrap the wealth tax.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wealthtax-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-wealthtax) Before it was replaced, the tax collected up to 1.5% of the wealth of French residents whose global worth exceeded €1.3m.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-208)

In February 2018, Macron announced a plan to offer [voluntary redundancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voluntary_redundancy "Voluntary redundancy") in an attempt to further cut jobs from the French civil service.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-209)

In December 2019, Macron announced that he would scrap the 20th-century pension system and introduce a single national pension system managed by the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-210) In January 2020, after weeks of public transport shutdown and vandalism across Paris against the new pension plan, Macron compromised on the plan by revising the retirement age.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-211) In February, the pension overhaul was adopted by decree using [Article 49 of the French constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_49_of_the_French_constitution "Article 49 of the French constitution").[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-212) However, on 16 March 2020, Macron announced that the draft legislation would be pulled as France went into [lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdown "COVID-19 lockdown") to slow the spread of [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19 "COVID-19").[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-213)

##### Terrorism

In July 2017, the Senate approved its first reading of a controversial bill with stricter anti-terror laws, a campaign pledge of Macron. The National Assembly voted on 3 October to pass bill 415–127, with 19 abstentions. Interior Minister [Gérard Collomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9rard_Collomb "Gérard Collomb") described France as being "still in a state of war" ahead of the vote, with the [1 October Marseille stabbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marseille_stabbing "Marseille stabbing") having taken place two days prior. The Senate then passed the bill on its second reading by a 244–22 margin on 18 October. Later that day Macron stated that 13 terror plots had been foiled since early 2017. The law replaced the [state of emergency in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency_in_France "State of emergency in France") and made some of its provisions permanent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-214)

The bill was criticized by human rights advocates. A public poll by _[Le Figaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Figaro "Le Figaro")_ showed 57% of the respondents approved of it even though 62% thought it would encroach on personal freedoms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-215)

The law gives authorities expanded power to search homes, restrict movement, close places of worship,[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-216) and search areas around train stations and international ports and airports. It was passed after modifications to address concerns about civil liberties. The most punitive measures will be reviewed annually and were scheduled to lapse by the end of 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-217) The bill was signed into law by Macron on 30 October 2017. He announced that starting 1 November, it would bring an end to the state of emergency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-218)

##### Civil rights

Visiting [Corsica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsica "Corsica") in February 2018, Macron sparked controversy when he rejected [Corsican nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsican_nationalist "Corsican nationalist") wishes for [Corsican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corsican_language "Corsican language") as an official language[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-219) but offered to recognize Corsica in the French constitution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-220)

Macron also proposed a plan to "reorganise" the [Islamic religion in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_France "Islam in France") saying: "We are working on the structuring of Islam in France and also on how to explain it, which is extremely important – my goal is to rediscover what lies at the heart of _[laïcité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%C3%AFcit%C3%A9 "Laïcité")_, the possibility of being able to believe as not to believe, in order to preserve national cohesion and the possibility of having freedom of conscience." He declined to reveal further information about the plan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-221)

##### Foreign policy and national defence

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Vladimir_Putin_and_Emmanuel_Macron_%282018-05-24%29_07.jpg/250px-Vladimir_Putin_and_Emmanuel_Macron_%282018-05-24%29_07.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vladimir_Putin_and_Emmanuel_Macron_(2018-05-24)_07.jpg) Regional County Municipality"),
